People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time getting past something in my head. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I keep thinking that if all my years working as an electrician weren’t enough to keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m… maybe I’m a danger to the people I work with, or even the people at home. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s the thought I can’t seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to sit in it too much, because I know that doesn’t help anybody, but it’s kind of always there. It’s this heavy mix of guilt and anger, like it’s sitting right in my chest most of the day.\n\nAnd, uh… when I look at my nephew, that’s when it hits me the worst. I think about how he lost his dad, and somehow he got stuck with me instead. I know that’s probably not the right way to look at it, but… that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m panicking all the time or anything. But there is this… heaviness, I guess. Like guilt and anger just kind of sitting in my chest most of the day.\n\nIt’s worse when I see my nephew. I start thinking about how he lost his dad and ended up with me instead, and… I know that’s not a helpful way to put it. But it’s hard not to feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing a lack of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… I haven’t been paying close attention to what I “enjoy” lately, if that makes sense.\n\nI still do what I need to do, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely stopped being myself. But… yeah, I guess I’d have to think about it more. What kind of activities do you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more like this heavy feeling that’s there most of the day.\n\nLike guilt and anger mixed together, sitting in my chest. And when I look at my nephew, that’s when it really gets to me… thinking he lost his dad and somehow got stuck with me instead. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make it sound dramatic, but I worry a lot about what could happen next.\n\nI keep thinking… if I’ve spent years working as an electrician and I still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? Like maybe I’m the risk. Maybe anybody who works with me, or even lives under my roof, is in danger because I’ll miss something again.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything. It’s just… that thought keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ll double-check things sometimes, but I figure a lot of people do that, especially with my kind of work. I haven’t noticed myself doing some ritual over and over or anything like that. If there’s something specific you’re asking about, I can try to think harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. I mean… with electrical work, checking things is just part of being careful, right?\n\nI guess sometimes it does feel like I’m trying to make sure nothing goes wrong, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or not. I don’t want to overstate it. It’s not like I have some set routine I have to do, at least I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel relieved after you do these double-checks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it relief, exactly. Maybe a little calmer for a minute, just because I can tell myself I looked at it.\n\nBut it’s not like some big weight comes off or anything. I’m sorry, I’m trying to answer that right… I just haven’t really paid attention to it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these double-checks are something you have to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I have to, no… not exactly.\n\nIt’s more like, if I’m already thinking about it, I’d rather just check than sit there wondering. But I wouldn’t say I’m forced to do it or that something terrible will happen if I don’t. At least, I don’t think so. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific thoughts or images that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but sometimes it does happen.\n\nI’ll be right in the middle of wiring a panel, or I’ll hear something like my nephew shutting the garage door, and all of a sudden it’s like… I’m back there. I can smell smoke, and I see my brother, and for a second the job site just kind of drops out from under me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. It’s not like I’m trying to think about it. It just shows up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks to get rid of a specific thought or image that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s to get rid of a specific image, no.\n\nIt’s more like… if something feels uncertain, I check it so I can stop wondering about it. I don’t think I’m doing it because one particular thought pops in and I’m trying to cancel it out or anything. At least I haven’t noticed that. Does that answer what you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Like maybe I’m the risk. Maybe anybody who works with me, or even lives under my roof, is in danger because I’ll miss something again.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything. It’s just… that thought keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ll double-check things sometimes, but I figure a lot of people do that, especially with my kind of work. I haven’t noticed myself doing some ritual over and over or anything like that. If there’s something specific you’re asking about, I can try to think harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. I mean… with electrical work, checking things is just part of being careful, right?\n\nI guess sometimes it does feel like I’m trying to make sure nothing goes wrong, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or not. I don’t want to overstate it. It’s not like I have some set routine I have to do, at least I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel relieved after you do these double-checks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it relief, exactly. Maybe a little calmer for a minute, just because I can tell myself I looked at it.\n\nBut it’s not like some big weight comes off or anything. I’m sorry, I’m trying to answer that right… I just haven’t really paid attention to it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these double-checks are something you have to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I have to, no… not exactly.\n\nIt’s more like, if I’m already thinking about it, I’d rather just check than sit there wondering. But I wouldn’t say I’m forced to do it or that something terrible will happen if I don’t. At least, I don’t think so. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific thoughts or images that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but sometimes it does happen.\n\nI’ll be right in the middle of wiring a panel, or I’ll hear something like my nephew shutting the garage door, and all of a sudden it’s like… I’m back there. I can smell smoke, and I see my brother, and for a second the job site just kind of drops out from under me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. It’s not like I’m trying to think about it. It just shows up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks to get rid of a specific thought or image that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s to get rid of a specific image, no.\n\nIt’s more like… if something feels uncertain, I check it so I can stop wondering about it. I don’t think I’m doing it because one particular thought pops in and I’m trying to cancel it out or anything. At least I haven’t noticed that. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things repeatedly helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little? I’m not totally sure I’d call it anxiety exactly.\n\nIt’s more like if I check, then I can tell myself, “Okay, that part’s done,” and I don’t have to keep wondering about it. But it’s not like I feel some huge wave of relief or anything. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m sorry, I’m trying to answer that right… I just haven’t really paid attention to it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these double-checks are something you have to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I have to, no… not exactly.\n\nIt’s more like, if I’m already thinking about it, I’d rather just check than sit there wondering. But I wouldn’t say I’m forced to do it or that something terrible will happen if I don’t. At least, I don’t think so. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific thoughts or images that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but sometimes it does happen.\n\nI’ll be right in the middle of wiring a panel, or I’ll hear something like my nephew shutting the garage door, and all of a sudden it’s like… I’m back there. I can smell smoke, and I see my brother, and for a second the job site just kind of drops out from under me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. It’s not like I’m trying to think about it. It just shows up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks to get rid of a specific thought or image that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s to get rid of a specific image, no.\n\nIt’s more like… if something feels uncertain, I check it so I can stop wondering about it. I don’t think I’m doing it because one particular thought pops in and I’m trying to cancel it out or anything. At least I haven’t noticed that. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things repeatedly helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little? I’m not totally sure I’d call it anxiety exactly.\n\nIt’s more like if I check, then I can tell myself, “Okay, that part’s done,” and I don’t have to keep wondering about it. But it’s not like I feel some huge wave of relief or anything. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these checks because you're trying to make something right or prevent something from going wrong in your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that exact way.\n\nI mean, I like things to be right, especially with work stuff, because it matters. But I don’t think I’m checking because I have some idea in my head that I have to fix, or because I’m trying to stop something from happening just by checking.\n\nIt’s more just… if I’m unsure, I look again. Maybe that sounds too simple, but that’s how it feels to me. Is that answering it okay?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I can smell smoke, and I see my brother, and for a second the job site just kind of drops out from under me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. It’s not like I’m trying to think about it. It just shows up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks to get rid of a specific thought or image that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s to get rid of a specific image, no.\n\nIt’s more like… if something feels uncertain, I check it so I can stop wondering about it. I don’t think I’m doing it because one particular thought pops in and I’m trying to cancel it out or anything. At least I haven’t noticed that. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things repeatedly helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little? I’m not totally sure I’d call it anxiety exactly.\n\nIt’s more like if I check, then I can tell myself, “Okay, that part’s done,” and I don’t have to keep wondering about it. But it’s not like I feel some huge wave of relief or anything. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these checks because you're trying to make something right or prevent something from going wrong in your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that exact way.\n\nI mean, I like things to be right, especially with work stuff, because it matters. But I don’t think I’m checking because I have some idea in my head that I have to fix, or because I’m trying to stop something from happening just by checking.\n\nIt’s more just… if I’m unsure, I look again. Maybe that sounds too simple, but that’s how it feels to me. Is that answering it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I don’t think there’s a special way I have to do it.\n\nIt’s more just looking again until I feel like I didn’t miss something. I don’t have, like, a set order or number of times, at least not that I’ve noticed. If I’m answering that right, I’d say it’s not really a specific ritual or anything.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time getting past something in my head. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I keep thinking that if all my years working as an electrician weren’t enough to keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m… maybe I’m a danger to the people I work with, or even the people at home. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s the thought I can’t seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to sit in it too much, because I know that doesn’t help anybody, but it’s kind of always there. It’s this heavy mix of guilt and anger, like it’s sitting right in my chest most of the day.\n\nAnd, uh… when I look at my nephew, that’s when it hits me the worst. I think about how he lost his dad, and somehow he got stuck with me instead. I know that’s probably not the right way to look at it, but… that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m panicking all the time or anything. But there is this… heaviness, I guess. Like guilt and anger just kind of sitting in my chest most of the day.\n\nIt’s worse when I see my nephew. I start thinking about how he lost his dad and ended up with me instead, and… I know that’s not a helpful way to put it. But it’s hard not to feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing a lack of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… I haven’t been paying close attention to what I “enjoy” lately, if that makes sense.\n\nI still do what I need to do, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely stopped being myself. But… yeah, I guess I’d have to think about it more. What kind of activities do you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more like this heavy feeling that’s there most of the day.\n\nLike guilt and anger mixed together, sitting in my chest. And when I look at my nephew, that’s when it really gets to me… thinking he lost his dad and somehow got stuck with me instead. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make it sound dramatic, but I worry a lot about what could happen next.\n\nI keep thinking… if I’ve spent years working as an electrician and I still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? Like maybe I’m the risk. Maybe anybody who works with me, or even lives under my roof, is in danger because I’ll miss something again.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything. I haven’t noticed myself doing some ritual over and over or anything like that. If there’s something specific you’re asking about, I can try to think harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. I mean… with electrical work, checking things is just part of being careful, right?\n\nI guess sometimes it does feel like I’m trying to make sure nothing goes wrong, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or not. I can smell smoke, and I see my brother, and for a second the job site just kind of drops out from under me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. It’s not like I’m trying to think about it. It just shows up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks to get rid of a specific thought or image that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s to get rid of a specific image, no.\n\nIt’s more like… if something feels uncertain, I check it so I can stop wondering about it. I don’t think I’m doing it because one particular thought pops in and I’m trying to cancel it out or anything. At least I haven’t noticed that. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things repeatedly helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little? I’m not totally sure I’d call it anxiety exactly.\n\nIt’s more like if I check, then I can tell myself, “Okay, that part’s done,” and I don’t have to keep wondering about it. But it’s not like I feel some huge wave of relief or anything. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these checks because you're trying to make something right or prevent something from going wrong in your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that exact way.\n\nI mean, I like things to be right, especially with work stuff, because it matters. But I don’t think I’m checking because I have some idea in my head that I have to fix, or because I’m trying to stop something from happening just by checking.\n\nIt’s more just… if I’m unsure, I look again. Maybe that sounds too simple, but that’s how it feels to me. Is that answering it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I don’t think there’s a special way I have to do it.\n\nIt’s more just looking again until I feel like I didn’t miss something. I don’t have, like, a set order or number of times, at least not that I’ve noticed. If I’m answering that right, I’d say it’s not really a specific ritual or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things repeatedly is something you must do to calm yourself down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I must do it, no. It’s more like… if I’m already unsure, checking makes it feel a little more settled, I guess.\n\nBut I don’t have that feeling like, “I can’t calm down unless I do this,” if that’s what you mean. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… I’m here because I’ve been having a hard time getting past something in my head. I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but I keep thinking that if all my years working as an electrician weren’t enough to keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m… maybe I’m a danger to the people I work with, or even the people at home. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s the thought I can’t seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to sit in it too much, because I know that doesn’t help anybody, but it’s kind of always there. It’s this heavy mix of guilt and anger, like it’s sitting right in my chest most of the day.\n\nAnd, uh… when I look at my nephew, that’s when it hits me the worst. I think about how he lost his dad, and somehow he got stuck with me instead. I know that’s probably not the right way to look at it, but… that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m panicking all the time or anything. If there’s something specific you’re asking about, I can try to think harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. I mean… with electrical work, checking things is just part of being careful, right?\n\nI guess sometimes it does feel like I’m trying to make sure nothing goes wrong, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or not. I don’t want to overstate it. It’s not like I have some set routine I have to do, at least I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel relieved after you do these double-checks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it relief, exactly. Maybe a little calmer for a minute, just because I can tell myself I looked at it.\n\nBut it’s not like some big weight comes off or anything. I’m sorry, I’m trying to answer that right… I just haven’t really paid attention to it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these double-checks are something you have to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I have to, no… not exactly.\n\nIt’s more like, if I’m already thinking about it, I’d rather just check than sit there wondering. But I wouldn’t say I’m forced to do it or that something terrible will happen if I don’t. At least, I don’t think so. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific thoughts or images that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but sometimes it does happen.\n\nI’ll be right in the middle of wiring a panel, or I’ll hear something like my nephew shutting the garage door, and all of a sudden it’s like… I’m back there. I can smell smoke, and I see my brother, and for a second the job site just kind of drops out from under me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. It’s not like I’m trying to think about it. It just shows up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks to get rid of a specific thought or image that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s to get rid of a specific image, no.\n\nIt’s more like… if something feels uncertain, I check it so I can stop wondering about it. I don’t think I’m doing it because one particular thought pops in and I’m trying to cancel it out or anything. At least I haven’t noticed that. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things repeatedly helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little? I don’t have, like, a set order or number of times, at least not that I’ve noticed. If I’m answering that right, I’d say it’s not really a specific ritual or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things repeatedly is something you must do to calm yourself down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I must do it, no. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s the thought I can’t seem to shake.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to sit in it too much, because I know that doesn’t help anybody, but it’s kind of always there. It’s this heavy mix of guilt and anger, like it’s sitting right in my chest most of the day.\n\nAnd, uh… when I look at my nephew, that’s when it hits me the worst. I think about how he lost his dad, and somehow he got stuck with me instead. I know that’s probably not the right way to look at it, but… that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge when you're not thinking about what happened to your brother?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m panicking all the time or anything. But there is this… heaviness, I guess. Like guilt and anger just kind of sitting in my chest most of the day.\n\nIt’s worse when I see my nephew. I start thinking about how he lost his dad and ended up with me instead, and… I know that’s not a helpful way to put it. But it’s hard not to feel that way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been experiencing a lack of interest in activities you normally enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that. I mean… I haven’t been paying close attention to what I “enjoy” lately, if that makes sense.\n\nI still do what I need to do, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely stopped being myself. But… yeah, I guess I’d have to think about it more. What kind of activities do you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more like this heavy feeling that’s there most of the day.\n\nLike guilt and anger mixed together, sitting in my chest. And when I look at my nephew, that’s when it really gets to me… thinking he lost his dad and somehow got stuck with me instead. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make it sound dramatic, but I worry a lot about what could happen next.\n\nI keep thinking… if I’ve spent years working as an electrician and I still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? Like maybe I’m the risk. Maybe anybody who works with me, or even lives under my roof, is in danger because I’ll miss something again.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything. It’s just… that thought keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ll double-check things sometimes, but I figure a lot of people do that, especially with my kind of work. I haven’t noticed myself doing some ritual over and over or anything like that. If there’s something specific you’re asking about, I can try to think harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. I mean… with electrical work, checking things is just part of being careful, right?\n\nI guess sometimes it does feel like I’m trying to make sure nothing goes wrong, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or not. I don’t want to overstate it. It’s not like I have some set routine I have to do, at least I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel relieved after you do these double-checks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it relief, exactly. Maybe a little calmer for a minute, just because I can tell myself I looked at it.\n\nBut it’s not like some big weight comes off or anything. I’m sorry, I’m trying to answer that right… I just haven’t really paid attention to it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these double-checks are something you have to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I have to, no… not exactly.\n\nIt’s more like, if I’m already thinking about it, I’d rather just check than sit there wondering. But I wouldn’t say I’m forced to do it or that something terrible will happen if I don’t. At least, I don’t think so. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific thoughts or images that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but sometimes it does happen.\n\nI’ll be right in the middle of wiring a panel, or I’ll hear something like my nephew shutting the garage door, and all of a sudden it’s like… I’m back there. I can smell smoke, and I see my brother, and for a second the job site just kind of drops out from under me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. It’s not like I’m trying to think about it. It just shows up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks to get rid of a specific thought or image that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s to get rid of a specific image, no.\n\nIt’s more like… if something feels uncertain, I check it so I can stop wondering about it. I don’t think I’m doing it because one particular thought pops in and I’m trying to cancel it out or anything. At least I haven’t noticed that. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things repeatedly helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little? I’m not totally sure I’d call it anxiety exactly.\n\nIt’s more like if I check, then I can tell myself, “Okay, that part’s done,” and I don’t have to keep wondering about it. But it’s not like I feel some huge wave of relief or anything. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these checks because you're trying to make something right or prevent something from going wrong in your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that exact way.\n\nI mean, I like things to be right, especially with work stuff, because it matters. But I don’t think I’m checking because I have some idea in my head that I have to fix, or because I’m trying to stop something from happening just by checking.\n\nIt’s more just… if I’m unsure, I look again. Maybe that sounds too simple, but that’s how it feels to me. Is that answering it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I don’t think there’s a special way I have to do it.\n\nIt’s more just looking again until I feel like I didn’t miss something. I don’t have, like, a set order or number of times, at least not that I’ve noticed. If I’m answering that right, I’d say it’s not really a specific ritual or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things repeatedly is something you must do to calm yourself down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I must do it, no. It’s more like… if I’m already unsure, checking makes it feel a little more settled, I guess.\n\nBut I don’t have that feeling like, “I can’t calm down unless I do this,” if that’s what you mean. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. I’m trying to answer that accurately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about the future are what's driving most of your distress?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes and no. I do worry about what could happen going forward, but the thing that really gets to me is this thought I can’t seem to get rid of.\n\nI came in because I keep thinking… if all my years doing electrical work couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? Like maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me, or even anybody living under my roof.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh, and I’m not saying it’s logical exactly. But it’s there a lot, and it’s hard to shake. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about potentially harming others in the future are the main reason you're feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably a big part of it.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m actually trying to hurt anybody or anything like that, because I’m not. But the thought I can’t get away from is… if I spent all those years doing electrical work and still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m not safe to be around. Like maybe people who work with me, or even people under my roof, could be at risk because of me.\n\nI know that sounds pretty extreme when I say it out loud. But yes, that’s the thought that keeps hitting me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I start thinking about how he lost his dad and ended up with me instead, and… I know that’s not a helpful way to put it. I mean… I haven’t been paying close attention to what I “enjoy” lately, if that makes sense.\n\nI still do what I need to do, and I don’t want to make it sound like I’ve completely stopped being myself. But… yeah, I guess I’d have to think about it more. What kind of activities do you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it sad exactly… I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is. It’s more like this heavy feeling that’s there most of the day.\n\nLike guilt and anger mixed together, sitting in my chest. And when I look at my nephew, that’s when it really gets to me… thinking he lost his dad and somehow got stuck with me instead. I know that probably sounds harsh, but that’s where my head goes.\n\nDoctor: Do you often worry about things that might happen in the future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. I try not to make it sound dramatic, but I worry a lot about what could happen next.\n\nI keep thinking… if I’ve spent years working as an electrician and I still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? Like maybe I’m the risk. Maybe anybody who works with me, or even lives under my roof, is in danger because I’ll miss something again.\n\nI know that probably sounds irrational. I’m not trying to be difficult or anything. It’s just… that thought keeps coming back.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or behaviors over and over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way I think you mean.\n\nI mean, I’ll double-check things sometimes, but I figure a lot of people do that, especially with my kind of work. I haven’t noticed myself doing some ritual over and over or anything like that. If there’s something specific you’re asking about, I can try to think harder.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks because you're trying to prevent something bad from happening?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it that strongly. I mean… with electrical work, checking things is just part of being careful, right?\n\nI guess sometimes it does feel like I’m trying to make sure nothing goes wrong, but I don’t know if that’s unusual or not. I don’t want to overstate it. It’s not like I have some set routine I have to do, at least I don’t think so.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel relieved after you do these double-checks?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it relief, exactly. Maybe a little calmer for a minute, just because I can tell myself I looked at it.\n\nBut it’s not like some big weight comes off or anything. I’m sorry, I’m trying to answer that right… I just haven’t really paid attention to it in that way.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like these double-checks are something you have to do?\n\nPatient: I don’t think it’s like I have to, no… not exactly.\n\nIt’s more like, if I’m already thinking about it, I’d rather just check than sit there wondering. But I wouldn’t say I’m forced to do it or that something terrible will happen if I don’t. At least, I don’t think so. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like there are specific thoughts or images that keep repeating in your mind?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t want to make it sound dramatic, but sometimes it does happen.\n\nI’ll be right in the middle of wiring a panel, or I’ll hear something like my nephew shutting the garage door, and all of a sudden it’s like… I’m back there. I can smell smoke, and I see my brother, and for a second the job site just kind of drops out from under me.\n\nI don’t know if that’s the kind of thing you mean. It’s not like I’m trying to think about it. It just shows up.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these double-checks to get rid of a specific thought or image that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say it’s to get rid of a specific image, no.\n\nIt’s more like… if something feels uncertain, I check it so I can stop wondering about it. I don’t think I’m doing it because one particular thought pops in and I’m trying to cancel it out or anything. At least I haven’t noticed that. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things repeatedly helps to reduce your anxiety?\n\nPatient: I guess… maybe a little? I’m not totally sure I’d call it anxiety exactly.\n\nIt’s more like if I check, then I can tell myself, “Okay, that part’s done,” and I don’t have to keep wondering about it. But it’s not like I feel some huge wave of relief or anything. At least, I don’t think so.\n\nIs that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're doing these checks because you're trying to make something right or prevent something from going wrong in your mind?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in that exact way.\n\nI mean, I like things to be right, especially with work stuff, because it matters. But I don’t think I’m checking because I have some idea in my head that I have to fix, or because I’m trying to stop something from happening just by checking.\n\nIt’s more just… if I’m unsure, I look again. Maybe that sounds too simple, but that’s how it feels to me. Is that answering it okay?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have to perform these checks in a specific way to feel okay?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I don’t think there’s a special way I have to do it.\n\nIt’s more just looking again until I feel like I didn’t miss something. I don’t have, like, a set order or number of times, at least not that I’ve noticed. If I’m answering that right, I’d say it’s not really a specific ritual or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like checking things repeatedly is something you must do to calm yourself down?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say I must do it, no. It’s more like… if I’m already unsure, checking makes it feel a little more settled, I guess.\n\nBut I don’t have that feeling like, “I can’t calm down unless I do this,” if that’s what you mean. At least I haven’t noticed it that way. I’m trying to answer that accurately.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about the future are what's driving most of your distress?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes and no. I do worry about what could happen going forward, but the thing that really gets to me is this thought I can’t seem to get rid of.\n\nI came in because I keep thinking… if all my years doing electrical work couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then what does that say about me? Like maybe I’m a danger to anybody who works with me, or even anybody living under my roof.\n\nI know that probably sounds harsh, and I’m not saying it’s logical exactly. But it’s there a lot, and it’s hard to shake. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about potentially harming others in the future are the main reason you're feeling this way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably a big part of it.\n\nI don’t want to make it sound like I’m actually trying to hurt anybody or anything like that, because I’m not. But the thought I can’t get away from is… if I spent all those years doing electrical work and still couldn’t keep my own brother safe, then maybe I’m not safe to be around. Like maybe people who work with me, or even people under my roof, could be at risk because of me.\n\nI know that sounds pretty extreme when I say it out loud. But yes, that’s the thought that keeps hitting me.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your worries about what could happen in the future are the main thing that's bothering you?\n\nPatient: I think… yes, mostly. Not just ordinary future worries, though.\n\nIt’s more this thought that keeps looping, like if all my years as an electrician still didn’t keep my brother safe, then maybe I’m dangerous to other people too — people I work with, or even people living in my house.\n\nI know that may sound like I’m putting too much on myself. I’m not trying to be dramatic. But that’s the thing that really bothers me and I can’t seem to shake it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your feelings of guilt and anger are more prominent than your worries about the future?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t want to overstate it, but the guilt and anger are there pretty much all the time.\n\nIt’s like this heavy mix sitting in my chest, and it gets worse when I look at my nephew. I keep thinking he lost his dad and somehow got stuck with me instead. That sounds awful to say, I know… but that’s where my head goes.\n\nSo yes, I guess the guilt and anger feel more right in front of me than just worrying about what might happen later.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
